Supplementary Material

Contents

S1
Preliminaries

S1.1
Notations

Spaces
We will denote by
\mathcal{H}
finite dimensional Hilbert spaces. The space of bounded linear operators on
\mathcal{H}
will be denoted as
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
. The set of positive operators on
\mathcal{H}
is denoted
\pos(\mathcal{H})\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
. Further, we define the set of positive, trace normalized operators on
\mathcal{H}
,

\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}):=\{X\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}):X\succeq 0,\Tr X=1\}.

(S1)

Then,
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
is compact and convex.

Norms
For a vector
x\in\ \mathbb{C}^{m}
and any
p\in[1,\infty)
we define the Schatten norms,

\|x\|_{p}:=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}|x_{i}|^{p}\right)^{1/p}

(S2)

For
p=\infty
we have,

\|x\|_{\infty}:=\max_{1\leq i\leq m}|x_{i}|

(S3)

For an operator
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
, we define the operator Schatten norm in terms of its singular values
\sigma(A)
as,

\|A\|_{p}:=\|\sigma(A)\|_{p}

(S4)

for any
p\in[1,\infty]
. We will frequently use the following facts:

1.

For any
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
and any operator
A
, we have,

\|A\|_{p}\geq\|A\|_{q}

(S5)

In particular,

\|A\|_{1}\geq\|A\|_{2}\geq\|A\|_{\infty}

(S6)

2.

For any
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
and any non-zero operator
A
,

\|A\|_{p}\leq\text{rank}(A)^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}\|A\|_{q}

(S7)

In particular,

\|A\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\text{rank}(A)}\|A\|_{\infty}

(S8)

We also note that, the
\infty-
norm obeys,

\|A\|_{\infty}=\sup_{x\neq 0}\frac{\|Ax\|_{2}}{\|x\|_{2}}=\sigma_{\text{max}}(A)

(S9)

we will denote
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
by just
\|\cdot\|
for convenience. Let
T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{q}}
be an order-
q
tensor with Frobenius norm

\|T\|_{F}^{2}:=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{q}}|T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{q}}|^{2}.

(S10)

For any bipartition of the tensor legs
A\subseteq\{1,\dots,q\}
and

B=A^{c}
, reshape the tensor into a linear map

T:\mathcal{H}_{A}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}

by grouping the indices in
A
and
B
into multi-indices

a=(i_{a_{1}},\dots,i_{a_{|A|}})
and
b=(i_{b_{1}},\dots,i_{b_{|B|}})
,
yielding matrix elements
T_{a,b}
. Then the Schatten-
2
norm of this
operator coincides with the Frobenius norm of the tensor,

\|T\|_{2}=\|T\|_{F}.

(S11)

We will also use the Hölder’s inequality for Schatten norms, where for each
(p,q)
satisfying
1/p+1/q=1
we have,

|\tr(A^\dagger B)|\leq\|A\|_{p}\|B\|_{q}

(S12)

Graphs
For a graph
G=(V,E)
, we define some useful notation. For any
v\in V
, let
\mathcal{N}(v)
denote its neigbors in
G
. For any edge
e=\{v,w\}\in E
, we will refer to it’s directed versions
\vec{e}=(v,w)
and
\overleftarrow{e}=(w,v)
. For vertices
v,w\in V
, let
d(v,w)
denote the graph distance (shortest path length) between
v
and
w
. For subsets
A,B\subseteq V
, define

d(A,B):=\min_{v\in A,w\in B}d(v,w).

For an edge
e=\{v,w\}
, we define, for any
u\in V
,

d(e,u):=\min\{d(v,u),d(w,u)\}

and similarly, for any
A\subset V
,

d(e,A):=\min_{u\in A}d(e,u).

Common symbols
We will use the following symbols throughout this work:

1.

D
. Bond dimension

2.

\Delta
. Graph Degree

3.

\delta
. Injectivity parameter

4.

\varepsilon:=1-\delta^{2}
. Deviation from maximal injectivity.

S1.2
(Strongly) injective tensors

We work throughout in finite dimension.
Let
\{\mathcal{H}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{l}
be finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces (the
l
virtual legs),
and let
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{phys}}
be the physical Hilbert space.
A single-site PEPS tensor with
l
virtual legs is a linear map

T:\bigotimes_{i=1}^{l}\mathcal{H}_{i}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{phys}}.

(S13)

Definition S1.1

(Injective tensor)
.

The tensor
T
is called
injective
if the induced map

T:\bigotimes_{i=1}^{l}\mathcal{H}_{i}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{phys}}

from virtual boundary to physical bulk is injective.

We perform a singular value decomposition

T=V\Sigma U^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{C}^{n_{p}\times n_{v}},

(S14)

where
n_{p}\geq n_{v}
by injectivity, where
U\in\mathbb{C}^{n_{v}\times n_{v}}
and
V:\in\mathbb{C}^{n_{p}\times n_{v}}
satisfy
U^{\dagger}U=\mathbbm{1}_{n_{v}}
(unitarity) and
V^{\dagger}V=\mathbbm{1}_{n_{v}}
(isometry), and
\Sigma=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{e})\in\mathbb{C}^{n_{v}\times n_{v}}
collects the singular values.

Normalize tensor such that the singular values satisfy,

\lambda_{\max}:=\max_{e}\lambda_{e}=1,\qquad\lambda_{\min}:=\min_{e}\lambda_{e}=\delta\in(0,1].

(S15)

We call
\delta
the
injectivity parameter
. Equivalently, this constraints the the
condition number
, defined as,

\kappa(T):=\frac{\max_{e}\lambda_{e}}{\min_{e}\lambda_{e}}

(S16)

to be
\kappa(T)=\delta^{-1}
.

Definition S1.2

(
\delta-
injective tensor)
.

We say that
T
is
\delta-
injective for
\delta\in(0,1]
if the condition number of the tensor satisfies
\kappa(T)\leq\delta^{-1}
.

By definition of injectivity, we have
\delta>0
. When
\delta=1
all singular values are unity and
T=VU^{\dagger}
itself is an isometry.

S1.3
Induced virtual superoperators

The associated
double-layer
(norm) tensor is obtained by contracting the
physical leg of
T
with its adjoint. Equivalently, for any bipartition of the virtual legs,

L\sqcup L^{c}
, the norm tensor defines a superoperator,

\Phi_{L\to L^{c}}:\mathcal{B}\!\left(\bigotimes_{i\in L}\mathcal{H}_{i}\right)\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}\!\left(\bigotimes_{j\in L^{c}}\mathcal{H}_{j}\right),\qquad\Phi_{L\to L^{c}}(X)=\sum_{a}K_{a,L\to L^{c}}\,X\,K_{a,L\to L^{c}}^{\dagger},

where
\{K_{a,L\to L^{c}}\}
are Kraus operators determined by
T
.

(a)
=
\Phi
(b)
\equiv
\Phi
(c)

If
T
is injective, the virtual superoperator can now be cast in the following form, for some disjoint pairing of legs
L\sqcup L^{c}
,

\Phi_{L\to L^{c}}(X):=\sum_{a}\lambda_{a}^{2}\,K_{a,L\to L^{c}}\,X\,K^{\dagger}_{a,L\to L^{c}},

(S17)

for
X\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{L})
, where
\{\lambda_{a}\}
are the singular values of
T
. We consider
T
normalized such that
\max_{a}\lambda_{a}=1
. Here
\{K_{a,L\to L^{c}}\}_{a}
are Kraus operators (determined by
U
and the bipartition) satisfying the
bistochastic
identities

\sum_{a}K^{\dagger}_{a,L\to L^{c}}K_{a,L\to L^{c}}=\mathbbm{\dim}(\mathcal{H}_{L^{c}})1_{L},\qquad\sum_{a}K_{a,L\to L^{c}}K^{\dagger}_{a,L\to L^{c}}=\mathbbm{\dim}(\mathcal{H}_{L})1_{L^{c}},

(S18)

which follow from the fact that
U
is a unitary. To see this, note that,

\sum_{a}U_{i_{1}\dots i_{\ell}}^{a}\bar{U}_{i_{1}^{\prime}\dots i_{\ell}^{\prime}}^{a}=\prod_{j}\delta_{i_{j}i_{j}^{\prime}}

Now, the Kraus operators are defined as,

K_{a,L\to L^{c}}:=[U^{a}]_{(i_{L})\to(i_{L^{c}})}

which satisfy completeness up to normalization. For instance,

\sum_{a}K^{\dagger}_{a,L\to L^{c}}K_{a,L\to L^{c}}=\sum_{a,i_{L^{c}}}[\bar{U}^{a}]_{(i_{L}^{\prime}),(i_{L^{c}})}[U^{a}]_{(i_{L}),(i_{L^{c}})}=\sum_{i_{L^{c}}}\delta_{i_{L},i^{\prime}_{L}}=\dim(\mathcal{H}_{L^{c}})\mathbbm{1}_{L}

Moreover, when
T
is
\delta-
injective with
\delta=1
(i.e., an isometry), the virtual superoperator is just a fully-depolarizing map (up to normalization).

S2
Projected entangled pair states

S2.1
Definitions

Consider a PEPS defined on a graph
G=(V,E)
with vertex set
V
and edge set
E
. Each vertex
v\in V
is associated with a local tensor
T_{v}
.

Definition S2.1

(PEPS)
.

A projected entangled pair state (PEPS) on a graph
G=(V,E)
with uniform virtual dimension
D
and physical dimension
d_{p}
is specified for each
v\in V
by a tensor

T_{v}:\bigotimes_{n\in\mathcal{N}(v)}\mathcal{H}_{(n,v)}\to\mathcal{H}_{\emph{phys}}\cong\mathbb{C}^{d_{p}},

where
\mathcal{N}(v)
denotes the set of neighbors of
v
, and each virtual space
\mathcal{H}_{(n,v)}\cong\mathbb{C}^{D}
.

We will consider graphs of uniform degree for notational simplicity, with
\mathcal{N}(v)=\Delta
for all
v\in V
.

Definition S2.2

(Injective PEPS)
.

We say a PEPS is
\delta
-injective if for every vertex
v\in V
the corresponding tensor
T_{v}
is
\delta-
injective.

S2.2
Belief Propagation

For each directed edge
\vec{e}=(v,w)
, we define a
bond-space

\mathcal{H}_{\vec{e}}\cong\mathbb{C}^{D}
. A message along edge
\vec{e}=(v,w)
is a positive operator
\mu_{\vec{e}}\in\pos(\mathcal{H}_{\vec{e}})
representing the message from
v
to
w
. Since messages in opposite directions may differ, we distinguish
\mu_{(v,w)}
from
\mu_{(w,v)}
.

Let
\vec{E}
denote the set of all directed edges. The collection of messages on all edges is represented as,

\bm{\mu}=(\mu_{\vec{e}})_{\vec{e}\in\vec{E}}\in\pos(G).

where we define
\pos(G)
to be the Cartesian-product space of positive matrices on each directed edge. Similarly, we denote with
\mathcal{K}_{G}
the product space of trace-one normalized positive matrices at each edge.

For each vertex
v\in V
and each neighbor
n\in\mathcal{N}(v)
, the tensor
T_{v}
induces a virtual superoperator

\Phi_{(v,n)}:\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\pos(\mathcal{H}_{(m,v)})\to\pos(\mathcal{H}_{(v,n)})

that takes the incoming messages from all neighbors of
v
except
n
and produces the outgoing message from
v
to
n
. In Kraus form,

\Phi_{(v,n)}\!\left(\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\mu_{(m,v)}\right)=\sum_{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}^{(v,n)}\,K^{(v,n)}_{\alpha}\,\left(\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\mu_{(m,v)}\right)(K_{\alpha}^{(v,n)})^{\dagger},

where
\{K^{(v,n)}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha}
are the Kraus operators corresponding to the bipartition that isolates leg
n
at vertex
v
, and
\lambda_{\alpha}^{(v,n)}>0
are the squared singular values of
T_{v}
in this bipartition. This is shown schematically as follows,

\Phi_{(v,n_{4})}
\mu_{(n_{1},v)}
\mu_{(n_{2},v)}
\mu_{(n_{3},v)}
\propto
\mu_{(v,n_{4})}

With the virtual superoperator, we define the message-passing maps of the PEPS.

Definition S2.3

(Message-passing maps of PEPS)
.

The
unnormalized message-passing map

\bm{f}:\pos(G)\to\pos(G)
is defined component-wise: for each directed edge
\vec{e}=(v,n)\in\vec{E}
,

[\bm{f}(\bm{\mu})]_{\vec{e}}:=f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}):=\Phi_{(v,n)}\!\left(\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\mu_{(m,v)}\right).

The
normalized message-passing map

\bm{F}:\mathcal{K}_{G}\to\mathcal{K}_{G}
is defined by

[\bm{F}(\bm{\mu})]_{\vec{e}}:=\frac{f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})}{\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})}.

The
fixed-points
of a PEPS will be fixed-points of the message passing equations, as defined in the following.

Definition S2.4

(Fixed point of PEPS)
.

A message configuration
\bm{\mu}_{\star}=(\mu_{\vec{e}})_{\vec{e}\in\vec{E}}\in\pos(G)
is a
fixed point
of the PEPS if for each directed edge
\vec{e}\in\vec{E}
, there exists
\lambda_{\vec{e}}>0
such that

f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})=\lambda_{\vec{e}}\mu_{\vec{e}}.

When all messages are normalized to unit trace (i.e.,
\bm{\mu}_{\star}\in\mathcal{K}_{G}
), a fixed point satisfies
\bm{F}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})=\bm{\mu}_{\star}
.

S2.3
Loop expansion

The contraction of the tensor network state,

\mathcal{Z}=\langle\psi|\psi\rangle

can be formally described as a Taylor series in terms of ‘loops’ on the network as described below. Herein, the zeroth order term is the BP approximation determined solely by the tensors and the fixed points.

Definition S2.5

(BP Approximation)
.

For each
v\in V
, the local contribution to the partition function is then given as,

Z^{(v)}:=\left[\underset{n\in\mathcal{N}(v)}{\bigotimes}\frac{\mu_{(n,v)}}{\sqrt{I_{vn}}}\right]\star T_{v},

(S19)

where
I_{vn}:=\Tr(\mu_{(v,n)}\mu_{(n,v)})
.

Now, we begin to define the excitations. The essential ingredient is the projector onto the non-BP subspace. Since the message tensors can be different in each direction, this is generally a non-orthogonal (w.r.t the Hilbert-Schmidt product) projector.

Definition S2.6

(Excitation projector)
.

For unit-trace operators
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
with
\Tr(X)=\Tr(X')=1
, define the excitation projector

\Pi_{X,X^{\prime}}^{\perp}(Y):=Y-\frac{\Tr(XY)}{\Tr(XX')}X^{\prime}.

(S20)

The excitations appear in
loops
, which are subgraphs with the following properties.

Definition S2.7

(Loop subgraph)
.

A
loop
is a connected subgraph
\ell=(W,F)
where
W
is the vertex set and
F
is the edge set, and every vertex has degree at least
2
. We call
|W|
the
weight
of the loop.

We denote the set of connected loops on the graph as
\mathcal{L}
. Two loops can be either compatible or incompatible, as defined below.

Definition S2.8

(Compatible loops)
.

Two loops
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}
are said to be
compatible
, written
\ell\sim\ell^{\prime}
, if they do not overlap; that is, they share no vertex or edge in the underlying graph. A family
\Gamma\subset\mathcal{L}
of loops is called
compatible
if every pair of distinct loops in
\Gamma
is compatible.

Given a loop
\ell
, the correction value
Z_{\ell}
is defined by the following tensor contraction.

Definition S2.9

(Loop activity)
.

Given a loop
\ell=(W,F)
, the
loop activity

Z_{\ell}
is defined as the contraction of the PEPS tensor network where:

1.

On each edge
e\in F
(edges inside the loop), insert the antiprojector
\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}
corresponding to the fixed point on that edge;

2.

On each edge
e\notin F
(edges outside the loop), insert the fixed point projector
\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}\otimes\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}
.

With this, we have the following series expansion for the tensor network contraction, from
37
;
15
. The tensor network is first normalized with respect to the BP approximation value, such that the BP approximation of the normalized object is unity. We will later show that given strong injectivity, this normalization is always meaningful. This leads to the following expansion.

Proposition S2.1

(Loop expansion)
.

The tensor network contraction admits the expansion

\mathcal{Z}=Z_{BP}\left(1+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Gamma\subset\mathcal{L}\\
\Gamma\emph{finite,compatible}\end{subarray}}\prod_{l\in\Gamma}Z_{l}\right)

(S21)

where the sum runs over all finite sets
\Gamma
of mutually compatible loops.

Owing to combinatorial growth of disconnected loops present in this expansion, this does not converge in general. We use techniques from cluster expansions to mitigate this problem, which is now reviewed.

Moreover, the loop tensors are always defined on the normalized tensor network, whose BP expectation value is unity. This ensures that the loop tensors are constructed from strictly local data. This locality extends to clusters, which we define now.

S2.4
Cluster expansion techniques

Definition S2.10

(Clusters)
.

Given the set of allowed excitations
\mathcal{L}
, a
cluster
is a collection of tuples

\mathbf{W}=\{(\ell_{1},\alpha_{1}),(\ell_{2},\alpha_{2}),\ldots,(\ell_{k},\alpha_{k})\}

where each
\ell_{i}\in\mathcal{L}
is a loop and
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
is the multiplicity of loop
\ell_{i}
in the cluster. The total number of loops in the cluster is
n_{\mathbf{W}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}
.

We define the cluster weight
|\mathbf{W}|=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}|l_{i}|
. We also denote
\mathbf{W}!=\prod_{i}\alpha_{i}!
. The support of a cluster is
\text{supp}(Z_{\mathbf{W}})=\cup_{l\in\mathbf{W}}\text{supp}(l)
. We denote the correction of the cluster
Z_{\mathbf{W}}
as the product of the loop corrections raised to their respective multiplicities:

Definition S2.11

(Cluster correction)
.

For a cluster
\mathbf{W}=\{(\ell_{1},\alpha_{1}),\ldots,(\ell_{k},\alpha_{k})\}
, the cluster correction is defined as

Z_{\mathbf{W}}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}Z_{\ell_{i}}^{\alpha_{i}}.

We call a cluster
\mathbf{W}

connected
if the interaction graph
G_{\mathbf{W}}
is connected, meaning there is a path between any two vertices in the interaction graph.

Definition S2.12

(Interaction graph)
.

Given a cluster
\mathbf{W}=\{(\ell_{1},\alpha_{1}),\ldots,(\ell_{k},\alpha_{k})\}
, the
interaction graph

G_{\mathbf{W}}=(V_{\mathbf{W}},E_{\mathbf{W}})
has
|V_{\mathbf{W}}|=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}
vertices, with loop
\ell_{i}
corresponding to
\alpha_{i}
vertices. There is an edge
(\ell,\ell^{\prime})\in E_{\mathbf{W}}
if the loops
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
are incompatible (
\ell\not\sim\ell^{\prime}
, i.e., they share edges), or if they are identical (
\ell=\ell^{\prime}
).

We define the following properties of a cluster.

Definition S2.13

(Properties of a cluster)
.

For a cluster
\mathbf{W}=\{(l_{1},\alpha_{1}),(l_{2},\alpha_{2}),\ldots\}
, we define the following:

(i)

The
cluster weight
(or “
order
”) is defined as

|\mathbf{W}|=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}|l_{i}|,

(S22)

where the number of edges in excitation
l
be denoted as
|l|
.

(ii)

The
support
of a cluster is,

\emph{supp}(Z_{\mathbf{W}})=\cup_{l\in\mathbf{W}}\emph{supp}(l)

(S23)

(iii)

The
correction
of the cluster
Z_{\mathbf{W}}
as the product of the loop corrections raised to their respective multiplicities:

Z_{\mathbf{W}}=\prod_{i}Z_{l_{i}}^{\alpha_{i}}.

(S24)

The following two results are from Ref.
37
, modifying the loop expansion into a cluster expansion in terms of the connected clusters on the tensor network.

Proposition S2.2

(Connected clusters only)
.

The free energy can be expressed as

\log\mathcal{Z}=\log Z_{BP}+\sum_{\emph{connected}\,\mathbf{W}}\phi(\mathbf{W})Z_{\mathbf{W}},

(S25)

where the sum runs over all connected clusters
\mathbf{W}
. The coefficient
\phi(\mathbf{W})
is given by

\phi(\mathbf{W})=\frac{1}{\mathbf{W}!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in G_{\mathbf{W}}\\
C\emph{connected}\end{subarray}}\sum_{(i,j)\in C}(-1)^{|E(C)|}

(S26)

The cluster expansion renders into an efficient algorithm given decay of loops.

Theorem S2.1

(Convergence of cluster expansion)
.

Assume there exists a constant
c>c_{0}:=\log(2e\Delta)+\frac{1}{2}
such that

|Z_{l}|\leq e^{-c|l|}

(S27)

then, the series for
\log Z
converges absolutely. Moreover, the error in truncating the series at order
m
, denoted
F_{m}
,

F_{m}=\log Z_{BP}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\emph{connected}\,\mathbf{W}\\
|\mathbf{W}|\leq m\end{subarray}}\phi(\mathbf{W})Z_{\mathbf{W}},

(S28)

is bounded by

\left|\log Z-{F}_{m}\right|\leq Ne^{-d(m+1)}

(S29)

where
d=c-c_{0}
,
\Delta
is the degree of the graph, and
N
is the number of vertices.

The main technical argument in the proof of Theorem
S2.1
is the following tail-bound on the decay of large clusters.

Lemma S2.1

(Tail bound for large clusters)
.

Consider a PEPS satisfying the loop decay condition
|Z_{\ell}|\leq e^{-c|\ell|}
for
c>c_{0}=\log(2e\Delta)+1/2
. For any region
A\subset V
and cutoff
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the contribution from all connected clusters with weight higher than
m
supported on
A
satisfies:

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\emph{connected}\,\mathbf{W}\\
\emph{supp}(\mathbf{W})\cap A\neq\emptyset\\
|\mathbf{W}|>m\end{subarray}}|\phi(\mathbf{W})Z_{\mathbf{W}}|\leq\order{|A| e^{-(c-c_0)(m+1)}}.

(S30)

We will use this in the later proofs that follow.

S2.5
Cluster expansion for observables and correlation functions

Before introducing these expansions, we define a key notation. As before,
\mathcal{L}
denotes the set of connected loops. Now, for any subset of vertices
A\subset V
, we denote
\mathcal{L}_{A}
to be the set of subgraphs which are min-degree two in
V/A
but can be degree one in
A
. With abuse of notation, we still refer to ‘loops’ in
\mathcal{L}_{A}
. That is,
\mathcal{L}_{A}
is the set of strings that loop in
V/A
but can terminate in
A
. For our purposes,
A
will always be some local region satisfying
|A|=\order{1}
.

The following are from Ref.
36
, providing a cluster expansion for local observables and correlation functions, with guarantees of convergence and clustering of correlations. We first review the multiplicative versions of the local observable expansions.

Proposition S2.3

(Cluster expansion for observables)
.

Let
|\psi\rangle
be a PEPS state on
G=(V,E)
. For a local observable
O_{A}
with support on region
A\subset V
and
\expectationvalue{O_A}\neq 0
, the expectation value admits a cluster expansion

\expectationvalue{O_A}=\expectationvalue{O_A}_{\text{BP}}\cdot\exp\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\emph{connected}\,\mathbf{W}\\
(\mathbf{W})\cap A\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\phi(\mathbf{W})Z^{O_{A}}_{\mathbf{W}}\right),

(S31)

where
\expectationvalue{O_A}_{\text{BP}}
is the BP approximation (computed using fixed point messages
\bm{\mu}_{\star}
), the sum is over connected clusters whose support overlaps
A
, and
\phi(\mathbf{W})
are combinatorial coefficients depending on the interaction graph structure. We have
Z^{O_{A}}_{\mathbf{W}}:=(Z^{A}_{\mathbf{W}}-Z_{\mathbf{W}})
where
Z_{\mathbf{W}}
and
Z^{A}_{\mathbf{W}}
denote the evaluation of the clusters on tensor networks
\mathcal{T}=\langle\psi|\psi\rangle
and
\mathcal{T}^{A}=\langle\psi|O_{A}|\psi\rangle
respectively after suitable BP normalization.

Given decay of loops (strings in
\mathcal{L}_{A}
), truncating this series at a finite cluster order
m
enjoys the following convergence guarantees.

Theorem S2.2
.

Given a local region
A\subset V
, assume decay of loops in
\mathcal{L}_{A}
on both
\mathcal{Z}=\langle\psi|\psi\rangle
and
\mathcal{Z}^{A}=\langle\psi|O_{A}|\psi\rangle
for a local observable with
\expectationvalue{O_A}\neq 0
. Then, truncating the cluster expansion for local expectation values

\expectationvalue{O_A}_{m}=\expectationvalue{O_A}_{\emph{BP}}\cdot\exp{\sum_{\substack{\emph{conn} \, \mathbf{W} \leftarrow\LL_A \\
\emph{supp}(\mathbf{W}) \cap A \neq\emptyset\\ |\mathbf W| \leq m}} \phi_{\mathbf{W}} (Z^A_{\mathbf{W}} - Z_{\mathbf{W}})}

(S32)

leads to a relative error
\delta_{m}=|\expectationvalue{O_A}-\expectationvalue{O_A}_{m}|/|\expectationvalue{O_A}|
bounded by

\delta_{m}\leq\order{|A| e^{-(c-c_0)(m+1)} }

(S33)

where
d=c-c_{0}=\order{1}
.

We also have the following additive version of the expansion for local observables.

Proposition S2.4
.

Let
\psi
be a state represented as a PEPS on
G=(V,E)
. For
A\subset V
, let
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{A}
denote the TN for
\langle\psi|\lambda O_{A}|\psi\rangle
with
\emph{supp}(O_{A})\subseteq A
, while
\mathcal{Z}^{A}
,
\mathcal{Z}
denote the TNs for
\langle\psi|O_{A}|\psi\rangle
and
\langle\psi|\psi\rangle
, respectively. Given a cluster
\mathbf{W}=\{(l_{1},\alpha_{1}),(l_{2},\alpha_{2}),...\}
, denote the cluster correction on
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{A}
,
\mathcal{Z}^{A}
, and
\mathcal{Z}
, by
Z_{{\mathbf{W}},\lambda}^{A},Z_{\mathbf{W}}^{A},Z_{\mathbf{W}}
, respectively. Also denote
\mathbf{W}_{A}
the subset of loops within
\mathbf{W}
which intersect
A
. Then

\expectationvalue{O}_{A}=\partial_{\lambda}\log\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{A}\mid_{\lambda=0}\,=\expectationvalue{O_A}_{BP}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathrm{conn}\,\mathbf{W}\leftarrow\mathcal{L}_{A}\\
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{W})\cap A\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\phi_{\mathbf{W}}Z_{\mathbf{W}}\sum_{l\in\mathbf{W}_{A}}\alpha_{l}\left[\frac{Z_{l}^{A}}{Z_{l}}-\expectationvalue{O_A}_{BP}\right].

(S34)

Given decay of loops, this also enjoys similar guarantees of convergence when
A
is a local region.

Theorem S2.3
.

Given a local region
A\subset V
, assume decay of loops in
\mathcal{L}_{A}
on both
\mathcal{Z}=\langle\psi|\psi\rangle
and
\mathcal{Z}^{A}=\langle\psi|O_{A}|\psi\rangle
. Then, truncating the cluster expansion (derivative version) for local expectation values (
Proposition S2.4
) of an observable with with
\|O_{A}\|=1
in a region
A\subset V
leads to an additive error bounded by

|\expectationvalue{O_A}-\expectationvalue{O_A}_{m}|\leq\order{m |A|e^{-d(m+1)}},

(S35)

where
d=c-c_{0}
.

The additive version of the expansion can straightforwardly be generalized to one for connected correlation functions, as summarized in the following.

Proposition S2.5
.

Let
\psi
be a state represented as a PEPS on
G=(V,E)
. Given local disjoint regions
A_{i}\subset V
, and observables
O_{A_{i}}
satisfying
\emph{supp}(O_{A_{i}})\subseteq A_{i}
, we have the following cluster expansion for the
p-
point connected correlation functions,

\expectationvalue{O_{A_1}\dots O_{A_p}}_{c}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\emph{conn.}\,\mathbf{W}\leftarrow\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{A}}\\
\emph{supp}(\mathbf{W})\cap A_{i}\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\phi_{\mathbf{W}}\partial_{\bm{\lambda}}Z^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{W},\bm{\lambda}}\Big|_{\bm{\lambda}=0}

(S36)

where we denote
\bm{\lambda}:=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{p})
,
\partial_{\bm{\lambda}}:=\prod_{i}\partial_{\lambda_{i}}
,
\mathbf{A}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{p})
and the cluster evaluation
Z^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{W},\bm{\lambda}}
is done over the network
\mathcal{Z}_{\bm{\lambda}}^{\mathbf{A}}:=\langle\psi|\exp(\sum_i O_{A_i}\lambda_i)|\psi\rangle
. The clusters
\mathbf{W}
are constructed over
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{A}}
, the set of connected sub-graphs required to be degree
\geq 2
everywhere except in all regions
A_{i}
.

This shows that the cluster expansion for
p-
point connected correlation functions must involve clusters intersecting all the local regions of concern.

Finally, we recall from
36
decay of loops in
\mathcal{L}_{AB}
for two local disjoint regions
A
and
B
suffices to show clustering of connected correlators
\expectationvalue{O_AO_B}_{c}
for bounded local operators
O_{A(B)}
.

Theorem S2.4
.

Decay of loops in
\mathcal{L}_{AB}
on the networks
\mathcal{Z}^{X}
for
X\in\{\emptyset,A,B,AB\}
ensures that bipartite connected correlators
\expectationvalue{O_AO_B}_{c}:=\expectationvalue{O_AO_B}-\expectationvalue{O_A}\expectationvalue{O_B}
for bounded local observables
O_{A(B)}
with
\emph{supp}(O_{A(B)})\subseteq A(B)
and
\|O_{A(B)}\|=1
cluster,

\Bigl|\expectationvalue{O_AO_B}_{c}\Bigr|\leq\order{e^{-d(A,B) / \xi} }

for a finite correlation length
\xi\leq\order{(1/(c-c_0))}
and
d(A,B)
being the graph distance.

We combine these results to show that decay of loops renders the cluster expansion into an efficient algorithm for computing properties of PEPS. Before we proceed, it is good to note the following:

(i)

Decay of loops is a bulk property. That is, for a loop
\ell
if
Z_{\ell}
(evaluated on
\mathcal{Z}
) decays, then so does
Z^{A}_{\ell}
(evaluated on
\mathcal{Z}^{A}
) for some local region
A
, since changing the contracting by some local amount can not alter the asymptotic decay.

(ii)

However, one does not straightforwardly imply the other (for instance, the loop activity of
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{A}/\mathcal{L}
vanishes on
\mathcal{Z}
but could be non-zero on
\mathcal{Z}^{A}
). Nonetheless, the proof technique we use to show decay of loops [in
Proposition S4.4
] proceeds via showing a decay of the building block of the loop excitations [in
Proposition S4.3
and
Lemma S4.4
] takes care of this subtle detail.

Theorem S2.5
.

For a PEPS satisfying the conditions of
Theorem S2.2
and
Theorem S2.4
, that is, satisfying decay of loops, we have that,

(i)

the norm
Z=\langle\psi|\psi\rangle
can be computed to
1/\poly(N)
multiplicative error in
\poly(N)
time

(ii)

local observables
\expectationvalue{O_A}=\langle\psi|O_{A}|\psi\rangle/\langle\psi|\psi\rangle
with
\expectationvalue{O_A}\neq 0
can be computed to multiplicative error
\epsilon
in
\poly(1/\epsilon)
time

(iii)

2
-point correlation functions can be computed to additive error
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\poly(N))
in
\poly(N)
time

Proof.

By
Theorem S2.1
, we have that the
m-
order approximation to the free-energy satisfies

\left|\log\mathcal{Z}-\tilde{F}_{m}\right|\leq Ne^{-d(m+1)}

(S37)

Hence, to ensure
\left|\log Z-\tilde{F}_{m}\right|<\order{1/\poly(N)}
we require
m=\Omega(\log{N})
. The complexity of computing a cluster of order
m
is
e^{\order{m}}
, which is
\order{\poly(N)}
for
m=\Omega(\log{N})
. Finally, note that additive error guarantee for
\log\mathcal{Z}
translates to a multiplicative error guarantee for
\mathcal{Z}
. This proves (i).

Now, to bound the error in computing local expectation values, we use
Theorem S2.2
. We have for a bounded
O_{B}
with
\expectationvalue{O_B}\neq 0
,

\Big|\frac{\expectationvalue{O_B}-\expectationvalue{O_B}_{m}}{\expectationvalue{O_B}}\Big|\leq\order{e^{-d(m+1)}}\leq\epsilon

(S38)

which can be ensured given
m=\order{\log{1/\epsilon}}
, again computable in
\poly{1/\epsilon}
time. This shows (ii).

Lastly, we compute correlation functions. Let
A
and
B
be the disjoint local regions of concern, with local operators
O_{A(B)}
with
\|O_{A(B)}\|=1
. If
d(A,B)=\Omega(\log{N})
then we have that
|\expectationvalue{O_AO_B}_{c}|\leq 1/\poly(N)
by
Theorem S2.4
and we trivially output
0
. For
d(A,B)=\order{\log{N}}
, we first note by
Proposition S2.5
that only clusters intersecting all the local regions appear in the expansion.
Second, we consider truncating the additive version of the correlator expansion of
Proposition S2.5
at cluster weight
m=d(A,B)+\order{\log{1/\epsilon}}
, leading to an error
\order{m^2 (|A|+|B|) e^{-dm}}
, which is (note that
(|A|+|B|)=\order{1}
,

\order{d(A,B)^2 \left(\log{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}\right)^2 \cdot e^{-d[d(A,B) + \log{1/\epsilon}]}}=\order{\log{N}\cdot\frac{1}{\poly(N)}}=\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\poly(N))

(S39)

since we already have
d(A,B)=\order{\log N}
and we choose
\epsilon=1/\poly(N)
. This can be computed at a cost of
e^{d(A,B)}\poly{1/\epsilon}
, which is
\poly(N,1/\epsilon)=\poly(N)
.
∎

S3
BP Fixed points of PEPS

S3.1
Existence of fixed points

We first show that, the virtual operator of an injective tensor does not map density matrices to zero.

Proposition S3.1
.

Assume the tensor
T
is
\delta-
injective with
\delta>0
. For any leg
i\in[\Delta]
, the Kraus
form for the map
\Phi_{[\Delta]_{\neq i}\to i}
can be chosen as

\Phi_{L_{\neq i}\to i}(Z)=\sum_{a}\lambda_{a}^{2}\,K_{a}ZK_{a}^{\dagger},\qquad\sum_{a}K_{a}^{\dagger}K_{a}=D\mathbbm{1}_{L_{\neq i}}.

(S40)

Then for all
X\in\mathcal{\mathcal{K}}(\otimes_{j\neq i}\mathcal{H}_{j})
,

\Tr f(X)\ \geq\ D\delta^{2}\ >\ 0.

(S41)

In particular
f(X)\neq 0
for all

X\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
, and the normalized map

F:\mathcal{\mathcal{K}}(\otimes_{j\neq i}\mathcal{H}_{j})\to\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{i}),\qquad F(X):=\frac{f(X)}{\Tr f(X)},

(S42)

is well-defined and continuous.

Proof.

Using the Kraus form and cyclicity of the trace,

\Tr f(X)=\Tr\!\left[\sum_{a}\lambda_{a}^{2}K_{a}XK_{a}^{\dagger}\right]=\Tr\!\left[\Big(\sum_{a}\lambda_{a}^{2}K_{a}^{\dagger}K_{a}\Big)\,X\right].

(S43)

Since
\lambda_{a}^{2}\geq\delta^{2}>0
and
\sum_{a}K_{a}^{\dagger}K_{a}=D\mathbbm{1}
,

\sum_{a}\lambda_{a}^{2}K_{a}^{\dagger}K_{a}\ \succeq\ \delta^{2}\sum_{a}K_{a}^{\dagger}K_{a}=\delta^{2}\,D\,\mathbbm{1},

hence

\Tr f(X)\geq\delta^{2}D\Tr(X)=\delta^{2}D.

(S44)

This proves (
S41
). Continuity of
F
is immediate from
continuity of
f
and the uniform lower bound on the denominator.
∎

Using
Proposition S3.1
, we can now show that the normalized message-passing map is well-defined and continuous.

Proposition S3.2
.

If the PEPS is
\delta
-injective with
\delta>0
, then for all
\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{K}_{G}
and all
\vec{e}\in\vec{E}
,

\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})\geq D\,\delta^{2}>0.

In particular,
\bm{F}
is well-defined and continuous on
\mathcal{K}_{G}
.

Proof.

Fix a directed edge
\vec{e}=(v,n)\in\vec{E}
. Using the Kraus representation and the trace lower bound from injectivity (as in
Proposition S3.1
),

\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})\geq\delta^{2}\sum_{\alpha}\Tr\!\left[(K_{\alpha}^{(v,n)})^{\dagger}K_{\alpha}^{(v,n)}\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\mu_{(m,v)}\right]=\delta^{2}D\prod_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\Tr\mu_{(m,v)}.

Since
\mu_{(m,v)}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{(m,v)})
, each
\Tr\mu_{(m,v)}=1
, yielding
\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})\geq D\,\delta^{2}
. The trace-normalized global map
\bm{F}:\mathcal{K}_{G}\to\mathcal{K}_{G}
is defined by

[\bm{F}(\bm{\mu})]_{\vec{e}}:=\frac{f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})}{\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})}.

Since for each edge
\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})\geq D\,\delta^{2}
, this is well-defined and continuous.

∎

Now, we recall Brouwer’s fixed-point theorem.

Theorem S3.1

(Brouwer
13
)
.

A continuous function on a nonempty finite-dimensional compact, convex set has a fixed point.

Since
\mathcal{K}_{G}
is a compact and convex finite-dimensional set, and
\bm{F}
is a continuous function mapping
\mathcal{K}_{G}
to itself, we thus get existence of fixed points.

Theorem S3.2

(Existence for injective PEPS)
.

If the PEPS is
\delta
-injective with
\delta>0
, then there exists a fixed point
\bm{\mu}_{\star}\in\mathcal{K}_{G}
such that
\bm{F}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})=\bm{\mu}_{\star}
.

Proof.

By
Proposition S3.2
,
\bm{F}
is a continuous map from the compact convex set
\mathcal{K}_{G}
to itself. Brouwer’s fixed-point theorem guarantees the existence of a fixed point.
∎

S3.2
Uniqueness of fixed points

Let
\Phi
be the virtual superoperator of a
\delta-
injective tensor. We will aim to perturb away from the
\delta=1
limit by expressing
\Phi=\Phi_{0}+\Delta\Phi
. Where,
\Phi_{0}
denotes the virtual superoperator in the maximally-injective case (
\delta=1
, all singular values
\lambda_{e}\equiv 1
). Then

\Phi_{0}\!\left(\bigotimes_{j\neq l}\mu_{j}\right)=\prod_{j\neq l}\Tr(\mu_j)\,\mathbbm{1}=\mathbbm{1},\qquad\text{for all }\mu_{j}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}).

(S45)

For general
\delta\in(0,1]
, decompose
\Phi=\Phi_{0}+\Delta\Phi
where

\Delta\Phi:=\Phi-\Phi_{0}=\sum_{e}(\lambda_{a}^{2}-1)K_{a}\,(\cdot)\,K_{a}^{\dagger}

captures the deviation from isometricity. We will use this form to show that the message-passing has a unique fixed point when this deviation is controlled.

Lemma S3.1
.

For the non-depolarizing perturbation
\Delta\Phi
in the virtual superoperator of a
\delta-
injective tensor for the leg bipartition
[\Delta]=L\cup L^{c}
, we have that

\|\Delta\Phi(A)\|_{1}\leq\dim(\mathcal{H}_{L^{c}})(1-\delta^{2})\|A\|_{1}

(S46)

for any
A\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{L})
.

Proof.

We have,

\Delta\Phi:=\Phi-\Phi_{0}=\sum_{a}(\lambda_{a}^{2}-1)K_{a}\,(\cdot)\,K_{a}^{\dagger}

(S47)

Let
E:=\sum_{a}(\lambda_{a}^{2}-1)\,K_{a}^{\dagger}K_{a}
and recall that
\sum_{a}K^{\dagger}_{a}K_{a}=\dim(\mathcal{H}_{L^{c}})\mathbbm{1}_{L}
. Since
\lambda_{a}^{2}\in[\delta^{2},1]
we get,

\|E\|_{\infty}\leq\dim(\mathcal{H}_{L^{c}})(1-\delta^{2}).

(S48)

For Hermitian
A\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{L})
, by Hölder’s inequality,

\|\Delta\Phi(A)\|_{1}\leq\|E\|_{\infty}\,\|A\|_{1}\leq\dim(\mathcal{H}_{L^{c}})(1-\delta^{2})\,\|A\|_{1}.

∎

For each edge
\vec{e}=(v,n)
, we decompose
\Phi_{(v,n)}=\Phi_{0}^{(v,n)}+\Delta\Phi^{(v,n)}
, where
\Phi_{0}^{(v,n)}
corresponds to the isometric case (
\delta=1
) and
\Delta\Phi^{(v,n)}
captures the deviation from isometricity. We finally combine these estimates to establish uniqueness of the fixed-point given
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{*}=1/(2\Delta-1)
(recall the definition of
\varepsilon:=1-\delta^{2}
).

Proposition S3.3

(Banach Contraction)
.

Let
\Delta\geq 2
and suppose the PEPS is
\delta
-injective with
\delta\in(0,1]
.
If

\delta^{2}>\frac{2(\Delta-1)}{2(\Delta-1)+1}\Leftrightarrow\quad\boxed{\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{*}:=\frac{1}{2\Delta-1}},

then the normalized map
\bm{F}:\mathcal{K}_{G}\to\mathcal{K}_{G}
is a Banach contraction on
(\mathcal{K}_{G},\|\cdot\|_{\max})
with contraction constant

q_{\Delta}(\delta):=\frac{2(\Delta-1)(1-\delta^{2})}{\delta^{2}}<1.

Consequently,
\bm{F}
has a unique fixed point
\bm{\mu}_{\star}\in\mathcal{K}_{G}
.

Proof.

Fix two message configurations
\bm{\mu},\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{K}_{G}
. We wish to estimate

\|\bm{F}(\bm{\mu})-\bm{F}(\bm{\nu})\|_{\max}=\max_{\vec{e}\in\vec{E}}\left\|\frac{f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})}{\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})}-\frac{f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\nu})}{\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\nu})}\right\|_{1}.

For each fixed edge
\vec{e}=(v,n)
, consider the contraction on
\Phi_{(v,n)}
, we aim to establish,

\left\|\frac{f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})}{\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})}-\frac{f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\nu})}{\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\nu})}\right\|_{1}\leq q_{\Delta}(\delta)\max_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\|\mu_{(m,v)}-\nu_{(m,v)}\|_{1},

where

q_{\Delta}(\delta):=\frac{2(\Delta-1)\,(1-\delta^{2})}{\delta^{2}}.

Since the incoming messages for edge
e=(v,n)
are
\{\mu_{(m,v)}:m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}\}
, and each of these is a component of
\bm{\mu}
, we have

\max_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\|\mu_{(m,v)}-\nu_{(m,v)}\|_{1}\leq\|\bm{\mu}-\bm{\nu}\|_{\max}.

The difference of the unnormalized component maps is

f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})-f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\nu})=\Phi_{(v,n)}\!\left(\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\mu_{(m,v)}-\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\nu_{(m,v)}\right).

Since
\Phi_{0}
is the depolarizing channel and all messages have unit trace, we have

\Phi_{0}\!\left(\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\mu_{(m,v)}-\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\nu_{(m,v)}\right)=\mathbbm{1}-\mathbbm{1}=0.

Therefore,

f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})-f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\nu})=\Delta\Phi\!\left(\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\mu_{(m,v)}-\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\nu_{(m,v)}\right).

Using the telescoping sum,

\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\mu_{(m,v)}-\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\nu_{(m,v)}=\sum_{k=1}^{\Delta-1}\left(\bigotimes_{i<k}\nu_{(m_{i},v)}\right)\otimes\bigl(\mu_{(m_{k},v)}-\nu_{(m_{k},v)}\bigr)\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{i>k}\mu_{(m_{i},v)}\right).

and applying
Lemma S3.1
with an application of triangle inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\|f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})-f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\nu})\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq D(1-\delta^{2})\sum_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n\}}\|\mu_{(m,v)}-\nu_{(m,v)}\|_{1}

(S49)

\displaystyle\leq(\Delta-1)D(1-\delta^{2})\|\bm{\mu}-\bm{\nu}\|_{\max}.

(S50)

Now, for positive operators
A,B
with traces
a:=\Tr A
and
b:=\Tr B
, we have the normalization inequality

\Big\|\frac{A}{a}-\frac{B}{b}\Big\|_{1}\leq\frac{2}{\min(a,b)}\,\|A-B\|_{1}.

(Proof: By triangle inequality and reverse triangle inequality,
\big\|\frac{A}{a}-\frac{B}{b}\big\|_{1}=\frac{1}{ab}\|bA-aB\|_{1}\leq\frac{1}{a}\|A-B\|_{1}+\frac{|a-b|}{ab}\|B\|_{1}\leq\frac{2}{a}\|A-B\|_{1}
.)

By
Proposition S3.1
,
\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})\geq D\delta^{2}
for all
\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{K}_{G}
. Therefore,

\left\|[\bm{F}(\bm{\mu})]_{\vec{e}}-[\bm{F}(\bm{\nu})]_{\vec{e}}\right\|_{1}=\left\|\frac{f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})}{\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})}-\frac{f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\nu})}{\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\nu})}\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{2}{D\delta^{2}}\|f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu})-f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\nu})\|_{1}\leq\frac{2(\Delta-1)(1-\delta^{2})}{\delta^{2}}\|\bm{\mu}-\bm{\nu}\|_{\max}.

Taking the maximum over all legs
l\in[\Delta]
,

\|\bm{F}(\bm{\mu})-\bm{F}(\bm{\nu})\|_{\max}\leq q_{\Delta}(\delta)\|\bm{\mu}-\bm{\nu}\|_{\max},\qquad q_{\Delta}(\delta):=\frac{2(\Delta-1)(1-\delta^{2})}{\delta^{2}}.

When
\delta^{2}>\frac{2(\Delta-1)}{2(\Delta-1)+1}
, we have
q_{\Delta}(\delta)<1
.
∎

Theorem S3.3

(Uniqueness and convergence to the fixed point)
.

Under the conditions of
Proposition S3.3
, an injective PEPS with
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{*}
has a unique fixed point
\bm{\mu}_{\star}\in\mathcal{K}_{G}
. Moreover, to achieve accuracy
\|\bm{\mu}^{(t)}-\bm{\mu}_{\star}\|_{\max}\leq\epsilon
, it suffices to take

t=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{\log(1/\epsilon)}{\log(\varepsilon_*/\varepsilon)}\right).

(S51)

Proof.

Consider the map
\bm{F}:\mathcal{K}_{G}\to\mathcal{K}_{G}
acting on the complete metric space
(\mathcal{K}_{G},\|\cdot\|_{\max})
. For
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{*}
, the map
\bm{F}
is a contraction with contraction factor
q_{\Delta}(\varepsilon)<1
, i.e.,

\|\bm{F}(\bm{\mu})-\bm{F}(\bm{\nu})\|_{\max}\leq q_{\Delta}(\varepsilon)\,\|\bm{\mu}-\bm{\nu}\|_{\max}\quad\forall\bm{\mu},\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{K}_{G},

(S52)

by
Proposition S3.3
. Since
(\mathcal{K}_{G},\|\cdot\|_{\max})
is complete, the Banach fixed-point theorem implies the existence and uniqueness of a fixed point
\bm{\mu}_{\star}\in\mathcal{K}_{G}
.

Applying the contraction bound with
\bm{\nu}=\bm{\mu}_{\star}
and iterating, we obtain

\|\bm{\mu}^{(t)}-\bm{\mu}_{\star}\|_{\max}\leq q_{\Delta}(\varepsilon)^{t}\,\|\bm{\mu}^{(0)}-\bm{\mu}_{\star}\|_{\max}.

(S53)

This shows exponential convergence to the fixed point. Since

q_{\Delta}(\varepsilon)=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{*}}\right),

the error after
t
iterations behaves, up to constants, as

\|\bm{\mu}^{(t)}-\bm{\mu}_{\star}\|_{\max}\lesssim\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{*}}\right)^{t}.

(S54)

Therefore, to achieve fixed-point error at most
\epsilon
, it suffices to choose
t
such that

\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{*}}\right)^{t}\lesssim\epsilon.

Taking logarithms yields

t=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{\log(1/\epsilon)}{\log(\varepsilon_*/\varepsilon)}\right).

(S55)

∎

S4
Loop Corrections

S4.1
Decay of single excitation

We now establish bounds on the decay of excitations from the fixed point found in
Theorem S3.3
. The key idea is to show that any fixed point must lie close to the normalized identity, and consequently the orthogonal projection away from the fixed point is well-approximated by the corresponding projection away from the identity. This ensures that the ‘depolarizing’ part of the virtual superoperator does not contribute significantly to the loop tensors. This is indeed a loose bound, and in practice one expects that loops will decay faster than the bounds proven hereon.

Starting from this section, we will assume that
\Delta
and
D
are all
\order{1}
constants. From there, we will not keep track of the coefficients of
\order{\varepsilon^2}
terms, which may be complicated functions of
(D,\Delta)
but the constant is always
\order{1}
. Later when we prove the convergence of any Taylor expansions, we can always choose a sufficiently small
\varepsilon
to account for the constant.

We first establish that the fixed-points of an injective tensor are
\order{\varepsilon}
close to the maximally-mixed state in trace-distance.

Proposition S4.1

(Fixed point proximity to identity)
.

Let
\bm{\mu}_{\star}
be the fixed point from
Theorem S3.3
. Then for each edge
\vec{e}\in\vec{E}
,

\|\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}-I_{1}\|_{1}\leq\frac{2(1-\delta^{2})}{\delta^{2}}=2\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}.

where
I_{1}=\mathbbm{1}/D
.

Proof.

Fix any vertex
v\in V
. Fix an outgoing directed edge from
v
, call it
\vec{e}
. Since
\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}
is a component of the fixed point, we have

\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}=\frac{f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})}{\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})}.

Write

f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})=\mathbbm{1}+R,\qquad R:=f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})-\mathbbm{1}.

We know that (i)
\|R\|_{1}\leq D(1-\delta^{2})
[by
Lemma S3.1
.] and (ii)
\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})\geq\delta^{2}D
[by
Proposition S3.1
]

Now write

\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}-I_{1}=\frac{f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})}{\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})}-\frac{\mathbbm{1}}{D}=\frac{\mathbbm{1}+R}{\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})}-\frac{\mathbbm{1}}{D}.

Applying the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\|\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}-I_{1}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|\frac{R}{\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})}\Big\|_{1}+\Big\|\mathbbm{1}\Big(\frac{1}{\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})}-\frac{1}{D}\Big)\Big\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\|R\|_{1}}{\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})}+\|\mathbbm{1}\|_{1}\,\frac{\big|\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})-D\big|}{\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})\cdot D}.

Using
\big|\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})-D\big|\leq\big|\Tr(R)\big|\leq\|R\|_{1}
and
\|\mathbbm{1}\|_{1}=D
, we obtain

\|\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}-I_{1}\|_{1}\leq\frac{2\|R\|_{1}}{\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{\mu}_{\star})}\leq\frac{2\,D(1-\delta^{2})}{\delta^{2}D}=\frac{2(1-\delta^{2})}{\delta^{2}}.

where we used
\|R\|_{1}\leq D(1-\delta^{2})
again. Thus,

\|\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}-I_{1}\|_{1}\leq 2\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}

∎

Next up, we show that given strong injectivity, the messages are full-rank, which is a technical step we need for ensuring that the BP normalization is well-conditioned.

Lemma S4.1

(Full rank messages)
.

Let
\mu\succeq 0
be an operator on a
D
-dimensional Hilbert space with
\Tr(\mu)=1

and let
I_{1}:=\frac{I}{D}

denote the trace-normalized identity. If

\|\mu-I_{1}\|_{1}<\frac{2}{D},

then
\mu
is full rank.

Proof.

Let
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{D}
be the eigenvalues of
\mu
. Since
\mu\succeq 0
and
\Tr(\mu)=1
, we have

\lambda_{i}\geq 0,\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{D}\lambda_{i}=1.

Since
I_{1}=I/D
, its eigenvalues are all equal to
1/D
. Since
[\mu,I_{1}]=0
, we have,

\|\mu-I_{1}\|_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\left|\lambda_{i}-\frac{1}{D}\right|.

We prove the contrapositive. Suppose that
\mu
is not full rank. Then at least one eigenvalue vanishes, say
\lambda_{j}=0
. Define

x_{i}:=\lambda_{i}-\frac{1}{D}.

Then

\sum_{i=1}^{D}x_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\lambda_{i}-1=0.

Hence the total positive part of the
x_{i}
’s equals the total absolute value of the negative part:

\sum_{i:x_{i}>0}x_{i}=\sum_{i:x_{i}<0}(-x_{i}).

Therefore,

\sum_{i=1}^{D}|x_{i}|=2\sum_{i:x_{i}<0}(-x_{i}).

Since
x_{j}=-1/D
, we obtain

\sum_{i:x_{i}<0}(-x_{i})\geq\frac{1}{D},

and thus

\|\mu-I_{1}\|_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\left|\lambda_{i}-\frac{1}{D}\right|=\sum_{i=1}^{D}|x_{i}|\geq\frac{2}{D}.

This proves the contrapositive: if
\mu
is not full rank, then

\|\mu-I_{1}\|_{1}\geq\frac{2}{D}.

Equivalently, if

\|\mu-I_{1}\|_{1}<\frac{2}{D},

then
\mu
is full rank.
∎

Now, we argue that the BP message normalization
I_{vw}
is perturbed away from the
\varepsilon=0
case by
O(\varepsilon^{2})
, given strong-injectivity. As a reminder, we defined the excitation projector given matrices
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
as

\Pi_{X,X^{\prime}}^{\perp}(Y):=Y-\frac{\Tr(XY)}{\Tr(XX')}X^{\prime}.

Lemma S4.2
.

For any edge
e=\{v,w\}\in E
, we have the normalization of the excitation projector (as in
Definition S2.6
)
I_{vw}:=\Tr(\mu_{(v,w),\star}\mu_{(w,v),\star})
obeys,

\big|I_{vw}-\frac{1}{D}\big|\leq 4\frac{(1-\delta^{2})^{2}}{\delta^{4}}=\order{\varepsilon^2}

(S56)

In addition,
I_{vw}
remains positive at sufficiently small
\varepsilon
.

\varepsilon<\frac{1}{1+2\sqrt{D}}\implies I_{vw}>0

(S57)

Proof.

Since
\Tr(\mu_{\star,\vec{e}})=\Tr(\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}})=\Tr(I_1)=1
, we have

\displaystyle\Tr(\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}})

\displaystyle=\Tr\!\Big([\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}-I_{1}+I_{1}]\,[\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}-I_{1}+I_{1}]\Big)

(S58)

\displaystyle=\Tr(I_1^2)+\Tr\!\Big(I_{1}[\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}-I_{1}]\Big)+\Tr\!\Big(I_{1}[\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}-I_{1}]\Big)+\Tr\!\Big([\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}-I_{1}][\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}-I_{1}]\Big)

(S59)

\displaystyle=\Tr(I_1^2)+\Tr\!\Big([\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}-I_{1}][\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}-I_{1}]\Big)

(S60)

Now, we have
\Tr(I_1^2)=1/D
and,

\Big|\Tr\!\Big([\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}-I_{1}][\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}-I_{1}]\Big)\Big|\leq\|\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}-I_{1}\|_{1}\|\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}-I_{1}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}-I_{1}\|_{1}\|\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}-I_{1}\|_{1}

where, we use Hölder’s inequality
|\Tr(AB)|\leq\|A\|_{1}\|B\|_{\infty}
and
\|B\|_{\infty}\leq\|B\|_{1}
. Further using Proposition
S4.1
, we get,

\Big|\Tr(\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}})-\frac{1}{D}\Big|\leq 4\frac{(1-\delta^{2})^{2}}{\delta^{4}}

(S61)

For small enough
1-\delta^{2}
, we are guaranteed that
\Tr(\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}})>0
, and the normalization in the excitation projector is meaningful. Substituting
\varepsilon=1-\delta^{2}
, the right-hand side becomes
4\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{(1-\varepsilon)^{2}}
. To ensure that the trace is strictly positive, we require the lower bound of this absolute value inequality to be greater than zero:

\frac{1}{D}-4\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{(1-\varepsilon)^{2}}>0

(S62)

Rearranging the terms and taking the square root of both sides, which is valid since
1-\varepsilon>0
, we obtain:

\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}>\frac{2\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\implies 1-\varepsilon>2\varepsilon\sqrt{D}

(S63)

Isolating
\varepsilon
, we find the strict positivity condition:

\varepsilon<\frac{1}{1+2\sqrt{D}}

(S64)

Thus, as long as
\varepsilon
satisfies this bound, we are guaranteed that
\Tr(\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}})>0
, and the normalization in the excitation projector is physically meaningful.

∎

Now, we show that the BP fixed point approximation is well conditioned (away from zero). This is important, because the cluster expansion requires normalizing the tensor network with the fixed point value. We obtain that for sufficiently small
\varepsilon<\order{1/D}
ensures well conditioned normalization.

Proposition S4.2

(Bound on BP approximation)
.

The local BP approximation at vertex
v
with degree
\Delta
,

Z^{(v)}:=\left[\bigotimes_{n\in\mathcal{N}(v)}\frac{\mu_{(n,v)}}{\sqrt{I_{nv}}}\right]\star T_{v},

(S65)

for a
\delta
-injective PEPS obeys,

\left|Z^{(v)}-D^{\Delta/2}\right|\leq D^{(\Delta/2)+1}\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}

(S66)

where
\varepsilon:=1-\delta^{2}
. In particular, we have that,

\varepsilon<\min\{\frac{1}{D},\frac{1}{1+2\sqrt{D}}\}\implies Z^{(v)}>0

(S67)

Proof.

We start by splitting
Z^{(v)}
into its numerator
N^{(v)}
and denominator
D^{(v)}
components. By definition, the numerator
N^{(v)}
evaluates to:

N^{(v)}:=\left[\bigotimes_{n\in\mathcal{N}(v)}{\mu_{(n,v)}}\right]\star T_{v}=1+\Tr\left[\mu_{(n_{*},v)}\Delta\Phi_{(v,n_{*})}\left(\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n_{*}\}}\mu_{(m,v)}\right)\right]

We can bound the trace term using Hölder’s inequality for Schatten norms (
\|AB\|_{1}\leq\|A\|_{\infty}\|B\|_{1}
) and the fact that the infinity norm is upper bounded by the 1-norm (
\|\mu_{(n_{*},v)}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\mu_{(n_{*},v)}\|_{1}=1
):

\Big|\Tr\left[\mu_{(n_{*},v)}\Delta\Phi_{(v,n_{*})}\left(\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n_{*}\}}\mu_{(m,v)}\right)\right]\Big|\leq 1\cdot\left\|\Delta\Phi_{(v,n_{*})}\left(\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n_{*}\}}\mu_{(m,v)}\right)\right\|_{1}

By
Lemma S3.1
, substituting
\dim(\mathcal{H}_{L^{c}})=D
and
1-\delta^{2}=\varepsilon
, we know that for any operator
A
:

\|\Delta\Phi(A)\|_{1}\leq D\varepsilon\|A\|_{1}

Applying this to our state, and using the fact that the trace norm of a product of valid normalized messages is
1
(i.e.,
\|\bigotimes\mu_{(m,v)}\|_{1}=1
), the deviation of the numerator from
1
,
\delta N=N^{(v)}-1
is bounded by:

|\delta N|\leq D\varepsilon

For the denominator
D^{(v)}
, we are given the bound on the local normalizations
I_{nv}
by
Lemma S4.2
. Substituting
\delta^{4}=(1-\varepsilon)^{2}
:

\Big|I_{nv}-\frac{1}{D}\Big|\leq 4\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{(1-\varepsilon)^{2}}=\order{\varepsilon^2}

This implies we can write
I_{nv}=\frac{1}{D}+\delta I_{nv}
where
|\delta I_{nv}|=\order{\varepsilon^2}
. Expanding the product of the denominators over the
\Delta
neighbors yields:

[D^{v}]^{-1}=\prod_{n\in\mathcal{N}(v)}I_{nv}^{-1/2}=D^{\Delta/2}\prod_{n\in\mathcal{N}(v)}(1+D\delta I_{nv})^{-1/2}=D^{\Delta/2}(1+\order{\varepsilon^2})

Assembling the numerator and the denominator, we get:

Z^{(v)}=D^{\Delta/2}(1+\delta N)(1+\order{\varepsilon^2})=D^{\Delta/2}(1+\delta N+\order{\varepsilon^2})

Subtracting the unperturbed expectation
D^{\Delta/2}
and applying our bound for
|\delta N|
yields the desired result:

\left|Z^{(v)}-D^{\Delta/2}\right|\leq D^{(\Delta/2)+1}\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}

Now
\varepsilon<1/D
, guarantees that the numerator is positive (this can also be seen by the fact that messages must be full rank by
Lemma S4.1
). From
Lemma S4.2
, we have that
\varepsilon<1/(1+2\sqrt{D})
guarantess that the denominator is positive. Thus, we have,

\varepsilon<\min\{\frac{1}{D},\frac{1}{1+2\sqrt{D}}\}\implies Z^{(v)}>0

(S68)

∎

One also notes that, positivity of the numerator of
Z^{(v)}
,

N^{(v)}:=\left[\bigotimes_{n\in\mathcal{N}(v)}{\mu_{(n,v)}}\right]\star T_{v}=\Tr\left[\mu_{(n_{*},v)}\Phi_{(v,n_{*})}\left(\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n_{*}\}}\mu_{(m,v)}\right)=\Tr\left[\mu_{(n_{*},v)}\sum_{a}\lambda_{a}^{2}K_{a}\mu_{m\neq n_{*}}K_{a}^{\dagger}\right]\right]

where we denoted
\mu_{m\neq n_{*}}:=\left(\bigotimes_{m\in\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{n_{*}\}}\mu_{(m,v)}\right)
can also be obtained by a full-rank condition on the messages. To see this, we first recall that
\varepsilon<1/D
ensures that the message tensors are full rank. Second, for each term in the sum, note,
\mu_{m\neq n_{*}}\succ 0\implies K_{a}\mu_{m\neq n_{*}}K_{a}^{\dagger}\succ 0
for
K_{a}\neq 0
, and thus

\Tr[\mu_{(n_{*},v)}K_{a}\mu_{m\neq n_{*}}K_{a}^{\dagger}]>0

since
\Tr(AB)>0
for two positive operators
A,B\succ 0
.

Now, we will use the fact that the fixed-point messages are
\order{\varepsilon}
close to the maximally mixed state to argue closeness of the projector to the orthogonal-projector onto the space of trace-less matrices.

Lemma S4.3

(Projection difference bound)
.

Given a fixed point
\bm{\mu}_{\star}
, denote its component on both directions of an edge
e
as
\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}
. Then, for any
Y
with
\|Y\|_{1}\leq 1
,

\|\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}(Y)-\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp}(Y)\|_{1}\leq g(\varepsilon,D,\Delta):=2(D+1)\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}

as long as

\varepsilon<\frac{1}{1+2\sqrt{D}}

Proof.

We will use the following:

1.

\Big|I_{e}-\frac{1}{D}\Big|\leq 4\frac{(1-\delta^{2})^{2}}{\delta^{4}}
[by
Lemma S4.2
]

2.

\|\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}-I_{1}\|_{1}\leq\frac{2(1-\delta^{2})}{\delta^{2}}
[by
Proposition S4.1
]

We can write

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}(Y)-\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp}(Y)

\displaystyle=\Big(Y-\frac{\Tr(\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}Y)}{I_{e}}\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}\Big)-\Big(Y-\frac{\Tr(I_1 Y)}{\Tr(I_1^2)}I_{1}\Big)

\displaystyle=\Tr(Y)I_{1}-\frac{\Tr(\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}Y)}{I_{e}}\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}},

adding and subtracting
\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}\Tr(Y)
gives,

\displaystyle\Tr(YI_1)-\frac{\Tr(\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}Y)}{I_{e}}\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}

\displaystyle\quad=\Tr(Y)[I_{1}-\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}]+\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}\left(\Tr(Y)-\frac{\Tr(\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}Y))}{I_{e}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\Tr(Y)[I_{1}-\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}]+\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}\left(\frac{\Tr(Y[I_e \mathbbm{1} - \mu_{\star,\vec{e}}])}{I_{e}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\Tr(Y)[I_{1}-\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}]+\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}\left(\frac{\Tr(Y[I_e \mathbbm{1} - I_1 + I_1 - \mu_{\star,\vec{e}}])}{I_{e}}\right)

We perform
|\Tr(Y[I_e \mathbbm{1} - I_1 + I_1 - \mu_{\star,\vec{e}}])|\leq|\Tr(Y[I_e \mathbbm{1} - I_1])|+|\Tr(Y[I_1 - \mu_{\star,\vec{e}}])|\leq\|Y\|_{1}\|(I_{e}-1/D)\mathbbm{1}\|_{\infty}+\|Y\|_{1}\|[I_{1}-\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}]\|_{\infty}\leq|(I_{e}-1/D)|+\|[I_{1}-\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}]\|_{1}
through a sequence of Hölder and Schatten norm inequalities. Thus,

\displaystyle\Big\|\Tr(YI_1)-\frac{\Tr(\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}Y)}{I_{e}}\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}\Big\|_{1}\leq\|I_{1}-\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}\|_{1}+\frac{1}{1/D-4\frac{(1-\delta^{2})^{2}}{\delta^{4}}}\left(4\frac{(1-\delta^{2})^{2}}{\delta^{4}}+\|I_{1}-\mu_{\star,\vec{e}}\|_{1}\right)

Plugging in the bounds from our previous propositions, we obtain:

\displaystyle\Big\|\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}(Y)-\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp}(Y)\Big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2(1-\delta^{2})}{\delta^{2}}+\frac{4\frac{(1-\delta^{2})^{2}}{\delta^{4}}+\frac{2(1-\delta^{2})}{\delta^{2}}}{1/D-4\frac{(1-\delta^{2})^{2}}{\delta^{4}}}.

Substituting the perturbation parameter
\varepsilon=1-\delta^{2}
, which implies
\frac{1-\delta^{2}}{\delta^{2}}=\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}
, yields:

\displaystyle\Big\|\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}(Y)-\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp}(Y)\Big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}+\frac{4\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{(1-\varepsilon)^{2}}+\frac{2\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}}{1/D-4\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{(1-\varepsilon)^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}+\frac{4D\varepsilon^{2}+2D\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}{(1-\varepsilon)^{2}-4D\varepsilon^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}+\frac{2D\varepsilon+2D\varepsilon^{2}}{1-2\varepsilon+(1-4D)\varepsilon^{2}}.

Finally, expanding this expression to first order in
\varepsilon
, we use
\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}=1+\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}
and
\frac{1}{1-2\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}}=1+2\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}
:

\displaystyle\Big\|\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}(Y)-\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp}(Y)\Big\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon(1+\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2})+(2D\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2})(1+2\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2})

\displaystyle=2\varepsilon+2D\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}

\displaystyle=2(D+1)\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}.

The condition on

\varepsilon<\frac{1}{1+2\sqrt{D}}

ensures that the normalization is positive.
∎

Uptil now, to require meaningful normalization with BP fixed point and closeness to the trace-less projector, we require,

\varepsilon<\min\{\frac{1}{D},\frac{1}{1+2\sqrt{D}}\}

(S69)

With this, we will establish that loop excitations decay with the size of the loop. To do that, we will first bound the ‘building block’ of a loop excitation in the following.

Proposition S4.3

(Excitation decay bound)
.

For any
v\in V
, the normalized building block with one edge excited satisfies,

\Big\|

\Big\|_{2}\leq\eta(\varepsilon,D,\Delta):=2D^{2-\Delta/2}(D+2)\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}.

where the red-line on the edge
\vec{v}=(v,n)
denotes the excitation projector
\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}
on that edge, and the vertex is normalized by the square root of BP expectation value [c.f.
Definition S2.5
].

Proof.

Fix a pair of conjugate edges
\vec{e},\overleftarrow{e}\in\vec{E}
. Now,
\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}\circ f_{\vec{e}}
is a superoperator from
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})^{\otimes{(\Delta-1)}}\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
. We will first aim to bound the numerator
\|\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}\circ f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{Y})\|_{1}
for arbitrary bounded
\bm{Y}
, and then add back in the bound on the BP normalization from
Proposition S4.2
.

For any matrix
A\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
, we have
\|A\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\text{rank}(A)}\max_{v:\|v\|_{2}=1}\|Av\|_{2}
and
\|A\|_{2}\leq\|A\|_{1}
. Now, the superoperator from
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})^{\otimes{(\Delta-1)}}\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
viewed has a matrix has dimensions
D^{2}
and
D^{2(\Delta-1)}
. Thus, the rank of this matrix is
\leq D^{2}
. This gives us,

\|\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}\circ f_{\vec{e}}\|_{2}\leq D\max_{\bm{Y}:\|\bm{Y}\|_{2}=1}\|\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}\circ f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{Y})\|_{2}\leq D\max_{\bm{Y}:\|\bm{Y}\|_{1}=1}\|\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}\circ f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{Y})\|_{2}

Since
\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp}\circ\Phi_{0}=0
(as
\Phi_{0}(\cdot)
is proportional to
\mathbbm{1}
), we have

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}\circ f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{Y})\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\|(\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}-\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp}+\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp})\circ f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{Y})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|(\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}-\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp})\circ f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{Y})\|_{2}+\|\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp}\circ f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{Y})\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\|(\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}-\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp})\circ f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{Y})\|_{2}+\|\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp}\circ\Delta\Phi(\bm{Y})\|_{2}.

We upper bound the first term as,

\displaystyle\|(\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}-\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp})\circ f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{Y})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|(\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}-\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp})\circ f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{Y})\|_{1}

(S70)

\displaystyle\leq g(\varepsilon,D,\Delta)\|f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{Y})\|_{1}

(S71)

\displaystyle\leq Dg(\varepsilon,D,\Delta)

(S72)

where we used
\|AB\|_{1}\leq\|A\|_{1}\|B\|_{\infty}\leq\|A\|_{1}\|B\|_{1}
and
Lemma S4.3
. For the upper bound on
\|f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{Y})\|_{1}
, we use
\|f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{Y})\|_{1}=\Tr f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{Y})\leq D\Tr\bm{Y}\leq D
.

For the second term, from
Lemma S3.1
by
\big\|\bm{Y}\big\|_{1}=1
, we get,

\big\|\Delta\Phi(\bm{Y})\big\|_{1}\leq D(1-\delta^{2}).

and thus (note
\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp}[Y]=Y-\Tr Y/D
),

\|\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp}\circ\Delta\Phi(\bm{Y})\|_{2}.\leq\|\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp}\circ\Delta\Phi(\bm{Y})\|_{1}\leq 2D(1-\delta^{2})

Combining these estimates,

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}\circ f_{\vec{e}}(\bm{Y})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq D\left(D\,g(\varepsilon,D,\Delta)+2D(1-\delta^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=D^{2}\left(2(1-\delta^{2})+g(\varepsilon,D,\Delta)\right)

\displaystyle=D^{2}\left(2\varepsilon+g(\varepsilon,D,\Delta)\right).

Now, we add back the BP normalization. To upper bound the entire expression, we require a lower bound on the denominator
Z^{(v)}
. From our previous bound in
Proposition S4.2
,
Z^{(v)}\geq D^{\Delta/2}(1-D\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2})
, which gives the upper bound:

\frac{1}{Z^{(v)}}\leq\frac{1}{D^{\Delta/2}\left(1-D\varepsilon\right)}+\order{\varepsilon^2}.

(S73)

We define our combined upper bound
\eta(\varepsilon,D,\Delta)
as:

\eta(\varepsilon,D,\Delta):=\frac{D^{2}\left[2\varepsilon+g(\varepsilon,D,\Delta)\right]}{D^{\Delta/2}\left(1-D\varepsilon\right)},

(S74)

where, as derived previously,

g(\varepsilon,D,\Delta)=2(D+1)\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}.

(S75)

Substituting this directly into the numerator bound:

\displaystyle D^{2}\left[2\varepsilon+g(\varepsilon,D,\Delta)\right]

\displaystyle=D^{2}\left[2\varepsilon+2(D+1)\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}\right]

\displaystyle=2D^{2}(D+2)\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}.

Next, we expand the geometric series from our inverse normalization bound for small
\varepsilon
:

\frac{1}{1-D\varepsilon}=1+D\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}.

(S76)

Finally, assembling
\eta(\varepsilon,D,\Delta)
and keeping only up to the linear term in
\varepsilon
:

\displaystyle\eta(\varepsilon,D,\Delta)

\displaystyle=\frac{D^{2}}{D^{\Delta/2}}\Big(2(D+2)\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}\Big)\Big(1+D\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}\Big)

\displaystyle=D^{2-\Delta/2}2(D+2)\varepsilon+\order{\varepsilon^2}.

∎

We now extend this to show that if the single-excitation vertex is
\order{\varepsilon}
, so is the multi-excitation vertex. This is in general non-trivial, but can be guaranteed by strong-injectivity as we show below.

Lemma S4.4
.

We have that an excitation block with any number of excited edges satisfies,

\Big\|

\Big\|_{2}\leq\eta(\varepsilon,D,\Delta)\implies~~\Big\|

\Big\|_{2}\leq\eta(\varepsilon,D,\Delta)\cdot(1+\order{\varepsilon})

Proof.

Note that inserting a projector
B
(satisfying
BB^{\dagger}\preceq\mathbbm{1}
) does not increase the Frobenius norm:

\|AB\|_{2}^{2}=\Tr(B^\dagger A^\dagger A B)=\Tr(BB^\dagger A^\dagger A)\leq\Tr(A^\dagger A)=\|A\|_{2}^{2}

However, note that
\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}(Y)
is not necessarily a projector. But, by
Lemma S4.3
we have for any bounded
Y
,

\|\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}(Y)-\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp}(Y)\|_{1}\leq\order{\varepsilon}

and thus, by a triangle inequality on the second excited leg,

\Big\|

\Big\|_{2}\leq\Big\|

\Big\|_{2}+\Big\|

\Big\|_{2}
.

where we used the fact that
\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp}
is a projector to upper bound the first term already, and, the dashed line denotes the difference
\Pi_{\mu_{\star,\vec{e}},\mu_{\star,\overleftarrow{e}}}^{\perp}(Y)-\Pi_{I_{1},I_{1}}^{\perp}(Y)
.
For the first term by
Proposition S4.3
,

\Big\|\hbox to45.75pt{\vbox to45.75pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 22.87445pt\lower-22.87445pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

and for the second term,

\Big\|\hbox to45.75pt{\vbox to45.75pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 22.87445pt\lower-22.87445pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{
